Still competitive for power transfer/ease; not lightest, not most elastic; prefer DIN-certified hybrid for resort laps; alpine-style heel more solid and efficient than any pin-heel tech binding; nicer on skin track than hybrids. Kingpin M-Werks ~100 g lighter, nearly identical.
- **Fritschi Tecton 13** — $700; stated 630 g (100 mm brakes). Pin toe efficiency + alpine-style heel; less jarring/more powerful than lighter bindings (toe elasticity and lateral release, heel vertical elasticity); doesn't match hybrids downhill but notable step up from full-pin; vs Kingpin: similar power, a bit lighter, adjustable toe release; Kingpin slightly more durable long-term. Mellow use → Vipec, ATK Raider.
- **G3 ION 12** — $664; 639 g (105 mm brakes). Simple, dependable pin binding; much better power transfer than old tech bindings (Dynafit Radical 1.0) though heavier bindings have more elasticity/power/smoother; intuitive, reliable; simple risers/touring mode; Pozidriv-serviceable; easy to clear of ice. Lighter with similar downhill → G3 ZED.
- **Dynafit ST Rotation 10** — $529; 626 g (105 mm brakes). Dynafit's most downhill-oriented line; rotating toe, adjustable vertical/lateral heel release, some elastic forward pressure; like Radical 2.0 it replaced, easier step-in, a touch lighter; simple transitions, great risers, fairly good downhill; middle of bell curve; no major issues over dozens of pairs; pivoting toe potential safety benefit. Also Rotation 14. Much lighter → Dynafit Ridge.
- **PLUM Karibou 14 Stopper** — €599; 595 g (85-100 mm brakes). PLUM's burliest; almost entirely metal; excellent heel risers; reliable optional brake; respectable power for full-pin; "elastic response system" spring under toe (8 mm elasticity for ski flex); reliable retention/release; brakeless Karibou 14 (stated 455 g) particularly compelling; Karibou 7 for lower release values.
- **Fritschi Vipec Evo 12** — $630; stated 580 g (100 mm brakes). Same toe as Tecton, lighter pin heel; skis very well for pin binding; very intuitive; easy risers; ski/walk transition without stepping out of toe; adjustable lateral toe release, smoother ride than most. More downhill → Tecton; more rugged → Karibou 14.
- **Marker Cruise 12** — $699; 482 g (105 mm brakes). Designed around ease of use; fully featured pin binding (simple brake, forward pressure compensation, multiple risers); very comfortable on skin track, risers easy and stay put; heel rotation effort less than most (except Ridge), without removing ski; good suspension for weight; +10 mm heel/toe delta a bit "tip-toey" but quick to adapt. Balanced.
- **Marker Alpinist Free 13** — $630; 463 g (105 mm brakes). Alpinist 12 with wider composite around toe/heel, higher max release, same mounting pattern; side-by-side testing: no noticeable difference vs Alpinist 12; good suspension, low delta, respectable power; single heel riser can flip out inadvertently. Ditch brakes ~100 g or brakeless Alpinist 12 (270 g).
- **Dynafit Ridge** — $599; 439 g. ~400 g with forward pressure compensation, integrated brakes, easy step-in; "Easy-Turn-Tech" heel greatly reduces wrestling when rotating between modes; no icing/durability issues so far; very easy, intuitive risers with ample heights; very good descent for weight, nice suspension, neutral +4 mm delta.
- **Salomon MTN Summit 12** — $725; 410 g (110 mm brakes). More downhill-oriented, fully featured vs MTN Pure (297 g w/o brakes); excellent risers, easy step-in; elastic forward pressure and adjustable lateral release; less vague power, fewer vibrations — not ATK Raider level but close, comparable to Alpinist Free 13/Ridge; brakes among burliest/most functional in ~400 g class.
- **G3 ZED 12** — $572; 358 g (no brakes, no leashes). Most of ION's features at nearly half the weight; same excellent risers, similar descent (harsher, less power than Tecton); comparable features to ATK Raider 12 (Raider more solid downhill, similar uphill); elastic forward pressure, big BSL range, multiple risers, adjustable vertical/lateral release. Heel-rotation struggles → Ridge, Cruise.
- **ATK Raider 12 AP** — $650; 352 g (97 mm brakes). Standout in 300-400 g class for years (rebranded by Moment, Hagan, Majesty, DPS, others); not as smooth/powerful as Tecton/Kingpin but no lighter binding skis notably better; fantastic risers, excellent build. Top pick for uphill efficiency + hard skiing. "Freeraider" adds gap-eliminating spacer; Raider EVO adds adjustable toe release (+~25 g); Raider SL minimal toe (−~12 g).
- **Tyrolia Almonte 12 PT** — $469; 327 g (105 mm brakes). Impressive debut; ~330 g with brakes; adjustable lateral heel release, fixed forward release (U-spring), gapless heel fit + optional spacer = slightly above-average power for class; functional mostly-internal brake (save ~65 g without); two easy heel risers; one of easiest toes to step into; Raider/Ridge a bit smoother; moderate price.
- **Dynafit Superlite 150** — $549; 176 g (no brakes, no leashes). Outperforms its name downhill; fairly fast big turns in forgiving snow; half the weight of bindings with similar (5-13) release; 200-lb reviewer in stiff boots drove big skis confidently; harsher on firm; ~6 mm delta. Adding brakes/BSL plates makes it ~300 g yet still fewer features.
- **Grizzly GR 98** — $649; 98 g (95 mm brake). Race/ski-mountaineering binding some reviewers use as ultralight BC setup; unique toe needs practice; remarkably solid for sub-100 g; few pre-releases; versions with stated release 8 or 10; GR 98 II adds 10 mm BSL adjustment; race-style heel with single low riser, lateral release in crash. Don't push limits. Higher release/more risers → Superlite 150.

**Climbing Skins — Mohair & Mix Plush**
- **Big Sky Mountain Products (BSMP) Endurance** — MSRP $169–229; mohair/nylon mix. Impressive glide for mix class with adequate grip on steep/icy tracks; Pomoca Free Pro glides slightly better and packs smaller; effective durable hardware for varied tip/tail shapes; consistent adhesive + glueless strips = reliable retention, still easy to rip with skis on. Very well-rounded.
- **Black Diamond GlideLite Mix STS** — ~$180. Mix plush glides better than nylon; burlier backing/adhesive; very comparable to Pomoca Tour Explore (BD glue stickier; Tour Explore packs smaller/lighter); latest lower-profile STS hardware less prone to snow creep, fits many shapes. More packable → Pomoca Free Pro, G3 Minimist Glide. Easy rip → others.
- **Contour Hybrid Mix** — ~$250. Hybrid adhesive holds most of the time yet far easier to rip than G3/BD glue (good for ripping with skis on); loses stickiness over time but revivable at home; hardware less secure than G3's but packs much smaller; slightly better grip than Free Pro/Minimist Glide; durable over many seasons. More glide → Hybrid Pure.
- **Contour Hybrid Pure** — ~$270. Mohair; easy transitions, holds well; revive tackiness by washing; glide/grip blend — less efficient than Pomoca Race Pro/Free Pro but slightly grippier; not as thin/packable; tail clips less secure than G3 (esp. twin tails). Also Hybrid Speed with slicker "Race" plush.
- **G3 Alpinist+ Glide** — ~$220. Most rugged G3 (sizable plastic inserts); same mix plush as Minimist Glide — good grip, glides much better than nylon; TPU tip doesn't pack small; excellent hardware/plush; not for low weight/packability/less-tacky glue; works on wide variety of skis.
- **G3 Minimist Glide** — ~$230. Sleeker, low-profile hardware; middle-of-road mix plush; secure tip hardware for odd/wide tips; very tacky glue (reviewers prefer Pomoca/BSMP/Contour adhesives); not for those hard on gear; prevents snow creep better than nearly all similarly thin skins; reduces bulk.
- **Kohla Vertical MixMohair** — ~$210. Used on many brands' pre-cut skins; thin/light between Pomoca Tour Explore and Free Pro 2.0 but stiffer — less packable, durable, less snow creep at ends; mid-pack grip/glide, above-average water resistance; Pomoca-like hardware; middle-ground adhesive. Alternative to BD GlideLite Mix (less tacky) and Minimist Glide (more durable).
- **Montana Adrenaline** — ~$240. Montana (known for ski-tuning machines) glueless "FusionTec" adhesion between Contour Hybrid and Pomoca in stickiness — very easy to rip yet stays on; mix plush; thickness/weight like Pomoca Tour Explore (maybe lighter); typical mix grip/glide. More grip → MontaNyl; thinner → Minimist Glide, Pomoca Free Pro.
- **Pomoca Free Pro** — ~$270. Designed for big freeride skis but go-to skin for several reviewers on any ski; very low-profile and efficient — packs smaller than most; glides really well, wide widths, respectable grip; Pomoca hardware works on nearly every ski; adhesive easier to rip than BD/G3 yet stickier than Contour Hybrid in tricky conditions; thin construction doesn't handle rocks/downed trees well. Phenomenal for glide and packability.
- **Pomoca Tour Explore** — MSRP $219–249; replaces Climb 2.0 as do-everything mix skin for middle-of-bell-curve users; glides and packs much better than nylon without much cost or grip loss; easier to rip without detaching. Very icy tracks/tight budget → BSMP Rover. One of most versatile, easy to recommend. More glide → Free Pro; cheaper → BSMP Endurance.
- **Pomoca Race Pro** — ~$190. Paul Forward's all-time favorite skin; only limited (narrow) widths and skimo attachments (unless uncut roll); most efficient glide here, excellent packability, adhesive holds yet peels easily; not durable, struggles on steepest/iciest tracks; supple. Wider alternative → Free Pro.

**Climbing Skins — Nylon Plush**
- **Big Sky Mountain Products Rover** — MSRP $139–199. Budget-friendly; heavier/bulkier but glides as well as any nylon skin, plenty of grip; real strength durability after seasons of abuse; Pomoca-like hardware. BSMP also offers professional re-glue service for any brand. More glide → Endurance.
- **Black Diamond Ascension Nylon STS** — ~$169. Decades-long nylon mainstay; glides slightly better than G3 Alpinist+ Universal; lighter and packs smaller than G3 nylon (more minimal hardware); sticky adhesive hard to rip with skis on. For grip, durability, affordability. Better glide → GlideLite Mix.
- **G3 Alpinist+ Universal** — ~$200. Reliable traction on snow and to skis; "fishscale" plastic tip meant to reduce trail-breaking friction (no significant difference noticed) but TPU tips help reduce snow creep; moderate weight; Rover more packable; for pack storage, firm-snow grip priority, skis with tips/tails that hold hardware poorly; tacky glue. Packable → Minimist.
- **Montana MontaNyl** — ~$189. Full nylon for grip/durability; traditional glue similar in stickiness to BD; solid all-round nylon plush, moisture-repelling, durable so far; packs a bit smaller than Alpinist+ Universal; hardware fine on most skis, less reliable on pointy/asymmetric tips. Also MontaMix (more efficient) and Adrenaline (less tacky).

**Blister Spectrums — Climbing Skins** (top → bottom; guide notes groups of adjacent skins as "similar"):
- Best Glide → Worst: Pomoca Race Pro & Free Pro (similar); Contour Hybrid Pure; Montana Adrenaline; BSMP Endurance; Pomoca Tour Explore; BD GlideLite Mix STS; G3 Alpinist+ Glide; G3 Minimist Glide; Contour Hybrid Mix; Kohla Vertical MixMohair; BSMP Rover; Montana MontaNyl; BD Ascension Nylon; G3 Alpinist+ Universal.
- Most Grip → Least: BD Ascension Nylon; Montana MontaNyl; BSMP Rover; G3 Alpinist+ Universal; G3 Minimist Glide; G3 Alpinist+ Glide; Pomoca Tour Explore; BSMP Endurance; BD GlideLite Mix; Montana Adrenaline; Kohla Vertical MixMohair; Contour Hybrid Mix; Pomoca Free Pro; Contour Hybrid Pure; Pomoca Race Pro.
- Most Packable (equivalent ski sizes) → Least: Pomoca Race Pro; Pomoca Free Pro; G3 Minimist Glide; BSMP Endurance; Montana Adrenaline; Contour Hybrid Mix; Contour Hybrid Pure; Pomoca Tour Explore; BD GlideLite Mix; BSMP Rover; Kohla Vertical MixMohair; Montana MontaNyl; BD Ascension Nylon; G3 Alpinist+ Glide; G3 Alpinist+ Universal.

### Snowboarding & Splitboarding (src ~12786-)
Section overview (Jed Doane photos, Furano / Daisetsuzan Japan): collection of reviewers' favorite snowboards, splitboards, bindings, splitboard skins — lots of time on most; what sets each apart. (Boards listed roughly by waist width; specs are STATED.)

**Snowboards**
- **Rossignol Juggernaut** — 297-253-297 (158); radii 6.7/7.3 m; 5% tip rocker / 90% camber / 5% tail rocker; 149-162 (158w, 161w); sintered 7500; paulownia/poplar + carbon/kevlar/fiberglass. Park-friendly twin with stout charging build; high-speed stability, edge hold, exceptional pop; centered for freestyle on firm/big features; set back for aggressive all-mountain. Like Whitespace Shaun White Pro but softer nose/tail → more practical off piste.
- **K2 Alchemist** — 301-253-295 (160); 7.98 m; tip rocker/camber; 154-164W; sintered 4001; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon tail "fork" + spectral braid + fiberglass. Highly capable aggressive freeride board deep pow to firm; stiffer directional, yet nimble at slow speed and in tight terrain vs equally stable boards; not most surfy/floaty but charges tracked snow, reliable landings; excellent edge hold, damp; for advanced/expert riders in big demanding terrain.
- **Whitespace Freestyle Shaun White Pro** — 296-256-296 (158); 8 m; camber; 146-162W; sintered UHMWPE; poplar/paulownia + carbon & fiberglass. Very stiff, precise true-twin full-camber freestyle board; balanced in air, extremely supportive landings; stiff nose deflects in variable snow (needs attention/aggression); low swing weight. Stiffer ends than Juggernaut. Competition-class park board for big features.
- **Nitro Squash** — 305-257-299 (159); radii 8.4/6.4 m; camber; 156, 159, 163; sintered speed formula HD; poplar/beech + fiberglass. Blends all-mountain versatility with surf feel; rails powerful turns (full camber, progressive sidecut, moderate swallowtail) — like Jones Hovercraft 2.0 on piste; exceptionally poppy; stubby swallowtail playful yet supportive (more than Weston Japow / Season Forma); more versatile than most powder swallows/fishes; nose floats, not chattery on firm. For swallowtail-curious riders prioritizing versatility. More balanced/freestyle → K2 Sky Pilot.
- **Weston Dream Machine** (new unisex, merges men's Range and women's Rise) — 306-258-306 (161); 7.8/8/7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152-164w; sintered 4000; poplar/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Many could quickly enjoy; directional twin, camber underfoot, medium-stiff — super playful with enough spine for tracks/variable; doesn't blast crud like Juggernaut but snappier, less demanding; intuitive edge hold. Float → Squash, Sky Pilot. Versatile with freestyle capability.
- **K2 Sky Pilot** (new; Sage Kotsenburg BC freestyle) — 308-259-298 (157); 7.5 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 148-163; sintered 4001; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon Y-beam + fiberglass. Not expert-only; lively core, spooned tip/tail, medium flex — easy turns, pivots in soft snow; subtle volume shift, 10 mm taper, still intuitive on hardpack, rides switch; rode 4-6 cm shorter than normal; not yet in really deep snow (optimistic); plows some chop, rewarding off side hits; not stiff. Compelling easy all-mountain freestyle daily driver.
- **Season Nexus** (board) — 302-260-296 (158); 7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-164 (159w, 162w); sintered 4001; poplar/paulownia + fiberglass. "Up for anything" middle of Season's small lineup; not most stable/snappy/floaty but makes "blah" days fun; lets experienced riders dictate turn style; easy for newer/intermediate riders (mellow torsional flex); predictable "medium" flex for side hits/jibbing. Coherent, un-niche. Quiver complement: Season Forma.
- **TahoeLab Directional** — 303-261-296 (158); 7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-181 (158W, 163W); sintered Durasurf; bamboo + carbon & fiberglass. Built with Freeride World Tour in mind; among most supportive, stable, confidence-inspiring boards tested; best edge grip here; tip/tail rocker more big-mountain versatile than old Amplid Pentaquark / Ride Commissioner; quite stiff yet snappy/playful, minimal chatter; handmade in Lake Tahoe, very durable even with rock strikes. Less aggressive → Season Nexus.
- **Weston Gnarnia** (new; solid & split) — 310-262-301 (162); radii 10/8.1/7.8/9.1 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-166W; sintered Durasurf 4001; poplar/paulownia/bamboo + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Unisex directional freeride blending outgoing Ridgeline and Riva; stiffer than Dream Machine but for playful freestyle-inspired freeriders; moderately stiff, impressive snap, lively takeoffs, supportive landings; floats well for class up to ~10" (25 cm); shines in tracked snow, soft chop, mixed; deep carves. Snappy daily driver with backbone.
- **Weston Japow** — 317-262-292 (159); radii 9/6.9 m; rocker/camber/flat; 154-169; sintered Durasurf 4001; bamboo/poplar + carbon tail stringers + fiberglass. Long-time reviewer favorite surf-inspired pow/carve board; traditional swallowtail encourages wide aggressive turns yet nimble in tight terrain; slower in open terrain than big-gun Amplid Snommelier but more maneuverable in trees; vs Rossignol XV Sushi: faster/more stable, less niche; playful, pops off hits. Top pick for fluid surf ride + adaptability; split version available.
- **Jones Hovercraft 2.0** — 311-263-294 (156); 9 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 144-164; sintered 8000 UHMWPE; poplar/paulownia + "Re-Up" stringers + flax & fiberglass. Longstanding powder-surf shape with sustainability materials — "Re-Up Tech" presses used snowboards into stringers (GEAR:30 ep. 270); big rockered nose floats/busts crud; tapered subtly split tail; mid stiffness, progressive toward short powerful tail; aggressive riders may find it lacking at speed in variable/airs. Approachable surfy quiver add, fun on groomers.
- **K2 Excavator** — 318-264-298 (154); radii 6.1/7.1 m; camber; 142-162; sintered 4001; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon stringers + carbon tail fork + fiberglass. Downsized volume-shifted board without losing high-speed composure; setback stance, stout carbon tail, 20 mm taper; plows chop, carves hardpack, bouncy in soft snow; ride shorter (reviewer down 7-8 cm), still stable on chewed groomers; back-foot-dominant style. For those worried volume-shifted boards are sketchy at speed.
- **Jones Storm Wolf** — 303-265-295 (162); 8.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 150-162; sintered 8000; paulownia/bamboo + flax & basalt stringers + carbon/innegra/fiberglass. Stiffened swallowtail freeride for big fast turns in pow and on piste; cuts heavy wet pow, snappy on features; speed-focused, very stable in long super-G carves; blend of big-gun swallows (Snommelier) and stubby volume-shifted (XV Sushi). For advanced/experts prioritizing big-turn stability.
- **Never Summer Proto Type 3** (new) — 312-265-312 (161X); 7.8 m; "Triple Camber Recurve" (camber-dominant feel, unlike NS's mostly rocker-dominant boards); 151-164X (incl. DF and X widths); sintered Durasurf XT 5501; poplar/paulownia/birch + carbon "matrix" & stringers + fiberglass. Stitched carbon stringers at tips/tails for torsional stiffness: easy to flex in middle, stiff laterally at ends; impressive damping — stable, chatter-free on firm and in chop for mid-stiff flex; true twin also good slightly set back. All-mountain freestyle for playful-aggressive riders.
- **Ride Warpig** — 316-265-306 (151); radii 5.4/6.5 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 142-158; sintered 4000; aspen/bamboo/paulownia + impact binding plates + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Volume-shifted freestyle-minded yet versatile; stubby mid-wide — stable at speed, easy through crud; skate-style ollies/spins; substantial low-rise tail more stable than pin-tailed rivals; mid-wide suits big feet, adds edge-angle stability; not quick edge-to-edge (fairly stiff); stiffer park board for big jumps. Rare middle ground.
- **Rossignol XV Sashimi** — 331-266-279 (160); radii 7.2/8.6 m; 30% rocker / 60% camber / 10% rocker; 152, 156, 160; sintered 7500; poplar + perimeter urethane strip + fiberglass. Most well-rounded of Xavier De Le Rue's collection; heavily tapered maneuverable freeride keeping XV Sushi's float/playfulness with much more high-speed dependability; big nose blasts chop, doesn't catch on rough hardpack; front-foot at speed or back-foot surfy in trees; substantial tail = snap and landing gear (not a freestyle board). More stability/freestyle → K2 Excavator.
- **Soul Stick Shift Elite** — 325-267-290 (159); radii 9/6.9 m; camber w/ 3D contouring; 159, 163; sintered 7700; poplar/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Unusual on-snow experience; excellent volume-shifted swallowtail for aggressive riders used to larger freeride boards; longer than similar boards — helps at speed and eases learning; "Elite" build one of dampest boards tested — nearly impossible to unsettle; set-back stance easy back-foot pivots; 3D nose floats/blows through chop; unwieldy in air and tight low-angle zones.
- **Jones Howler** — 312-267-304 (160W); 8.6 m; camber; 152-163W; sintered 8000; paulownia/bamboo/poplar + carbon/flax stringers + koroyd tip inserts + fiberglass. Freeride/freestyle for aggressive steep riders who still throw tricks; Xavier De Le Rue input; high-speed stability + agility and pop; subtle taper, buoyant nose; stiff full camber = edge hold, pop, landing support; composed across groomers, chop, crud; playful, supportive tail rides switch; carves well but not deepest trench-digger (Head E.B.I. LYT, Nitro Squash).
- **Season Forma** — measured 319-267-308 (153); 7 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 144, 153, 159; sintered 4000; poplar/paulownia + fiberglass. Middle ground between nimble pow slashers and long directional chargers; one of most approachable surf-inspired boards; fast in deep snow, ample float, playful; similar to Weston Japow (Forma surfier, easier to throw, slightly slower in very deep); decent groomer edge hold. Great entry to swallowtails. Stiffer/stable → Storm Wolf.
- **United Shapes Deep Reach** — 322-272-302 (162); 8.6 m; tip rocker/camber; 149-162; sintered 2100; poplar/paulownia + carbon weave & stringers + fiberglass. Powder-focused but transitions to chop, firm groomers; wide blunted nose stays up, supportive tail not sinky; less surfy than stubby volume-shifted (Storm Chaser) but far more planted at speed; excels in tracked chop with stiffness/damping plus torsional give; less aggressive carver than Japow but predictable. Pow shape that works after noon.
- **Ride Superpig** — 301-270-291 (154); radii 5.6/6.9 m; tip rocker/camber; 142-158; sintered 4000; aspen/bamboo/paulownia + carbon stringers + carbon & fiberglass. Warpig's sharper, more disciplined sibling; stubby shape floats well but less easy in deep snow than traditional (Nexus) or tapered (XV Sashimi) boards; very sharp snappy carves; more comfortable at speed than Warpig; freestyle-capable for aggressive riders; trades skate-style maneuverability for firm-snow grip. Warpig more accessible/playful slow.
- **Head E.B.I. LYT** — 311-272-306 (161); radii 8.6/7.4 m; rocker/camber/flat/camber/rocker; 143-161; sintered; "lightweight wood" + Hexagonal Core tip/tail inserts + graphene + fiberglass. Versatile, accessible freeride; balances float and agility with uncompromised edge hold; wide rockered nose + swallowtail float in light/moderate snow; soft nose folds in dense heavy snow at speed (Howler/Storm Wolf better); truly shines carving groomers; medium flex, supportive tail for carving-oriented intermediate-advanced resort riders.
- **Jones Storm Chaser** — 324-275-304 (152); 6.9 m; tip rocker/camber; 142-157; sintered 8000; paulownia/bamboo + flax & basalt stringers + fiberglass. Agile dynamic "pow-groomer" board less specialized than stubby rivals; size down; fast, easy to plane, maneuverable on low-angle slopes vs Forma/XV Sushi; not as fast on bottomless days or stable in chop as Storm Wolf/Japow; remarkable on-piste carver (camber between bindings), like Squash but easier to slash. Encourages more carving.
- **Rossignol XV Sushi** — 340-276-282 (145W); radii 8/9.5 m; 30% rocker / 60% camber / 10% rocker; 144, 145W; sintered 7500; poplar + urethane strips + fiberglass. Maximalist short-and-stubby: 340 mm nose, ~60 mm taper, 88 mm tail, 145 cm; agile powder surfer; ride front-foot in pow, calm in soft chop but loses composure in firm crud at speed (vs Japow/Forma); not versatile but blast on groomers — extreme width = effortless carving without toe/heel drag... (cont.)
  (XV Sushi cont.) unique quiver addition, pair with stiff binding for power across width. More versatile surf shapes → XV Sashimi, Excavator, Squash.
- **K2 Special Effects** — 333-284-313 (152); 6.8 m; tip rocker/flat; 140-152; sintered 4000; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Volume-shift taken to extreme: massive width, stiff flex, very short tail, 8 mm nose spoon; extremely easy initiation, maneuverable, surfy; nimble and buoyant — perfect for tight Hokkaido pow trees; less tapered/more symmetric than XV Sushi — more intuitive for less-directional riders; not damp in crud; fun to carve, passable in packed pow/dust-on-crust. More accessible → Head E.B.I. LYT.

**Splitboards** (stated specs; weights where given)
- **Jones Solution Split** — 311-254-298 (164); 9.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 154-169w; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + flax & basalt stringers + fiberglass. Well-rounded soft-snow split; floats very well, big turns, accessible in tight trees; advanced riders find speed limit in firm/variable (edge hold, chatter) — making it better for less experienced riders; easy kick turns, dual-serrated edges grip when split. Lighter/stiffer → Ultralight Solution.
- **Prior Khyber Split** — 314-256-287 (160); 8.0 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 150-170; sintered 4001 Durasurf; yellow cedar/maple/red cedar + carbon (custom). Powder-focused yet versatile; wide nose/narrower tail floats, reduces leg fatigue; poppy in light "XTC" build (heavier fiberglass build available); great for pillows/features/trees; not as fast in deep snow as Never Summer Swift / Chimera Unicorn Chaser but more playful/balanced; lighter than Venture Paragon, similar performance; camber underfoot; customizable graphics/build/tune.
- **Jones Ultralight Stratos Split** — 305-256-295 (159); 7.5 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 156, 159, 161W; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + koroyd + carbon & fiberglass; ~3000 g @159. 3D-spooned tip/tail minimize catchiness of light splits; responsive on hard snow; middle-of-road with deep-snow and BC freestyle bias; livelier than Solution/Hovercraft 2.0; not a charger — softer than most carbon splits; standard Stratos Split cheaper but much heavier.
- **Chimera Unicorn Chaser** — 312-257-284 (159); 8.9 m; rocker/flat/camber; 159; sintered 4001 Durasurf; aspen/poplar + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Surf silhouette; fluid/floaty, pivots under front foot, camber under back foot bites on hardpack; tail cutout fast in pow (like Swift), nimble; not as surfy as stubby Rossignol Sushi but more stable/versatile; uphill slightly better than Swift (lighter, narrower nose, camber). For surf-curious but wary riders.
- **Weston Backwoods Carbon Split** — 312-259-294 (160); radii 7.7/6.9/8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152-167W; sintered 4000 electra; paulownia/bamboo + vertical aluminum stringers + carbon; ~2900 g @160. Do-it-all; floats, edges, composed on serious descents, light uphill; moderately directional; stout flex for variable/speed; 1-split quiver contender vs Venture Paragon, Prior Khyber (stiffest of the three). Surfier → Jones Ultralight Hovercraft. Solid grip, easy kick turns; one of most dependable at speed.
- **Stranda Shorty Backcountry** — 313-260-299 (164); 8.6 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 159-169 (164W); sintered Durasurf 4001; ash/poplar + paulownia tip inserts + carbon stringers + fiberglass; ~3250 g @159. Not a stubby surfer — balanced, versatile; long wide nose floats, rises after drops (better landings than short-tailed boards); softer nose than Unicorn Chaser/Swift — less for charging tracked/firm; camber good for carving/slashing pow, slush, corn. Accessible soft-snow surfer at reasonable weight.
- **Weston Japow Split** — 317-262-292 (159); 9/6.9 m; rocker/camber/flat; 154-169; sintered Durasurf 4001; bamboo/poplar + carbon tail stringers + fiberglass; ~3270 g @159. Classic swallowtail: 25 mm taper, forward stance in deep snow, deep rear sidecut quick turns; light but durable; reviewer kept swapping back to it in Hokkaido; predictable on cat tracks/variable; Weston's recommended skins lacked coverage on steep sketchy ascents; check tail clip fit with metal notches. One of favorite all-round pow boards.
- **Never Summer Swift Split** — 319-263-299 (162); 8.32 m; camber/rocker/camber ("Original Rocker Camber"); 157, 162, 167; sintered Durasurf XT 5501; paulownia/poplar/birch + carbon & fiberglass; 3650 g @162. Powder surf meets freeride charging; retro shape surfy/floaty; longer tail/heavier weight = more stability at speed/variable; handles airs/landings well; not as nice skinning — rocker underfoot problematic on icy tracks. Skinning priority → Ultralight Hovercraft.
- **Venture Storm Split** — 314-265-305 (162); 7.92 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 152-167; sintered p-tex; poplar/ash + fiberglass. Venture's BC workhorse (alpine faces, trees, variable); burly durable builds, lighter over years; 9 mm taper; floats, turns easily in trees, reliable on skin track (no slippage seen on some rockered splits); Paragon more freestyle; Euphoria surfier for deepest days. Carbon version available.
- **Cardiff Goat Pro Carbon Split** — 301-265-290 (162); 11 m avg; rocker 40%/camber 60%; 150-166; sintered graphite; poplar/paulownia + titanal stringers + carbon; ~3085 g @162. Unusually poppy yet stable; light, durable, predictable in rough; slightly tapered/setback — float and stability, balanced in air, great edge hold; one of poppier splits tested. Versatile for fast all-terrain riders using natural features. "Enduro" construction heavier/softer/cheaper.
- **Venture Paragon Split** — 315-265-307 (159); 7.61 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 149-168; sintered p-tex; poplar/ash + fiberglass. Dependable in huge range of BC situations; modestly directional, freestyle-friendly; particularly good in chop/crud; maneuverable in trees, spins/switch, grip for traverses/icy couloirs; not light (carbon version lighter) but very durable; damp for moderate flex; more stable than Jones Stratos/Solution, more freestyle-friendly than Backwoods/Powgoda. Safe investment.
- **Jones Ultralight Hovercraft 2.0 Split** — 316-267-299 (160); 9.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152, 156, 160; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + koroyd + carbon & fiberglass; ~2700 g @160. High-end light construction + directional carving shape; large spooned nose floats, surfy, catch-free; no durability/performance sacrifice noticed; flex stiff enough for speed yet easy to throw around; "Traction Tech" serrations both sides when split; similar to discontinued favorite Amplid Millisurf. Balanced freeride split for uphill efficiency + carving + float.
- **Cardiff Powgoda Pro Carbon Split** — 314-272-294 (158); 8.4 m; rocker 50%/camber 50% (mostly nose rocker); 148-162; sintered graphite; poplar/paulownia + titanal stringers + carbon; 2984 g @158. Light, surfy, dependable float with everyday versatility; moderately tapered tail; not as agile as stubby surfers; progressive sidecut: back-foot for tight trees, front-foot for big turns. More freestyle → Goat Pro Carbon. For long powder-hunting days.
- **Voile Skyline** — 310-272-299 (162); 9.5 m; rockered tip/camber; 154-172; sintered; paulownia + carbon & fiberglass; ~3490 g @162. Inspires confidence in consequential terrain; edge hold, stability, yet pivots and floats in soft snow for advanced riders; most impressive in poor conditions — very damp, minimal chatter; not easy to pivot on firm, long radius favors long fast turns; like a damper, stabler, wider Jones Solution. For riders who find splits sketchy on steep firm lines.

**Snowboard Bindings**
- **Nidecker OG Supermatic** — sizes M (5-8), L (8.5-10.5), XL (11-13); nylon, fiberglass; flex 3/5. Hinged highback: step down to lock and tighten straps; release lever drops highback/loosens straps; very efficient; precision riders still tweak buckles; OG not very stiff — Carbon Supermatic to be tested. Quick transitions without special boots.
- **Jones Mercury FASE** — flex 4/5; nylon, fiberglass, carbon hanger; M (8.5-10.5), L (11-14). "Fast Entry System": highback pivots back, step through elongated straps, step down to engage, a couple clicks; no special boots; supportive yet tweakable, rides like a traditional two-strap; vs Supermatic: similar efficiency, lighter, lower profile, more natural feel. For intermediate-advanced riders.
- **Arbor Cypress** — S/M (7-9), M/L (9-11), L/XL (11-13+); aluminum, fiberglass, plant-based strap plastic, EVA; medium-stiff. Lots of customization; comfortable plant-based straps; more responsive than playful; X-shaped baseplate + foam preserves board flex (like NOW/YES SkateTech); highly adjustable; impressive durability for modest price.
- **Jones Apollo Pro** — flex 5/5; nylon, fiberglass, carbon, flax; M (7.5-10), L (10+). Precision and power; NOW/YES SkateTech chassis (center pivot) reduces fatigue, increases edging response; stiff asymmetric carbon/flax highback; smooth edge-to-edge; great with stiff/wide boards; ankle straps "freeride" vs "surf" mode; swappable softer bushings. Daily driver for stiff-board fans.

**Splitboard Bindings**
- **Spark R&D Arc ST Pro** — medium flex; aluminum, carbon-reinforced nylon, pebax; XS (<7) to L (10.5+); ~560 g/binding (M). Spark "Pro" bindings favorites of most reviewers; trims weight with few compromises; no durability red flags after seasons; carbon-infused asymmetric highback slightly stiffer than Arc ST, still surfy do-everything; more compact in-house pucks this season; Ibex ST Pro Crampon ~120 g each. Moderate flex, light uphill.
- **Voile Light Speed** — S (4-7.5), M (8-10.5), L (11+); aluminum, steel, nylon; medium; ~750 g/binding. Competitive with category leaders; "Quick Lock" bracket similar to Spark Tesla T1; solid metal pins mounted to board (less chance to fall out than Spark's binding-embedded pins); many screws — repairable but more to monitor; more built-in adjustability; slightly more prone to icing; some prefer its risers/straps; comparable to Spark Surge ST in weight/downhill.
- **Spark R&D Surge ST** — stiff; aluminum, fiber-reinforced nylon, plastic; XS-L; ~630 g/binding. Stiffer, burlier counterpart to Arc ST; "Pillow Line" semi-elastic straps conform, shed snow, more durable than stitched straps; Tesla T1 + "ST" hardware excellent usability/durability; responsive for steep, playful, fast; less stiff than Karakoram Straightline/X Carbon but more versatile, better value. Lighter → Surge ST Pro.
- **Spark R&D Surge ST Pro** — stiff; aluminum, carbon-reinforced nylon, pebax; XS-L. Flagship; ~70 g/foot lighter than Surge ST without durability/performance loss; overmolded thermoplastic ST brackets, Whammy Bars, Ibex crampons (smoother, longer rotation); asymmetric "Rip 'N' Flip" highbacks; strong enough for big boards, not harsh; no major durability issues in seasons; go-to for several reviewers.

**Splitboard Climbing Skins**
- **Kohla Peak Mixmohair Splitboard Universal** — 65% mohair/35% nylon; waterproof "Fiber Seal" backing prevents soaking/icing; less tacky "Smart Glue" easy rips; adaptive to nearly any split; hardware more versatile than BD standard; great grip-to-glide — gripped better than G3 nylon Universal on ice and pow; secure clips but some tail-clip durability issues. Among favorite split skins (not for gear abusers).
- **G3 Splitboard+ Universal** — 100% nylon; grip/durability; latest hardware fits varied tails; good if you like BD nylon grip but had hardware issues; tip/tail connections more reliable than Kohla Peak, poorer glide; "Craft" program occasional limited-run Glide/Speed variants at discount; "Splitboard LT" more minimal hardware.
- **Black Diamond Ascension Splitboard STS** — nylon benchmark for grip/durability; slightly better glide than G3 Splitboard+ Universal; multidirectional-adjust tail clip eases cutting, secure (less versatile than G3 for odd shapes). More glide/compact → Kohla Peak, BD GlideLite Mix split.
- **Jones Nomad** (made by Pomoca) — universal trim-to-fit and precut for Jones splits after 2016; 70% mohair/30% nylon, grip/glide like Kohla Peak; less efficient than G3 Glide on low-angle but grippier steep/icy; Nomad Pro adds Pomoca Glide treatment; precut tail clips need board tail notch — works great with Ultralight Hovercraft 2.0; no glue issues with proper care; thin, light, packs smaller than BD Ascension/G3 Universal.

### Apparel (src ~13989-)
Section overview (Paul Forward / Sascha Anastas photos): apparel matters (soaked shells, steamy pants); favorites from light breathable touring pieces to burly resort garments; subset of site reviews; first two pages tested by female reviewers, following by male reviewers (most pieces come in men's/women's/unisex).

**Female-reviewer tested**
- **Flylow Avery Jacket & Foxy Insulated Bib** — $450 / $440; 821 g (jacket L), 717 g (bib L); jacket 2L Lux laminate (20K/20K) + 80-g & 100-g PrimaLoft Black ECO recycled; bib 2L Tactic + 40-g PrimaLoft. Reliable weather protection and warmth at good price; favorite for chilly resort days; generous vents, many pockets, rugged (hundreds of days); mid-spectrum fit. Uninsulated BC → new Flylow Domino Jacket / Siren Bib. For those who run cold or want simple layering.
- **The North Face Summit Series Verbier Jacket & Bib** — $700 / $600; 785 g / 779 g (M); 3L Gore-Tex (75Dx40D recycled face). Flagship freeride kit; lighter fabric for resort but packable for BC without feeling fragile; mid breathability, reliable protection; loads of features (pockets, big hood, tall collar, vents); relaxed long fit, great ROM. Strafe/Rab breathe better.
- **Strafe Meadow Jacket & Willow Half Bib** — $629 / $469; 520 g / 545 g (S); jacket 3L eVent DValpine (40D); bib 3L Recon ELITE stretch (63D). Double duty in/out of resort; lots of venting, slightly above-average breathability; practical in cold/stormy with midlayers; flattering, feature-rich, sleek; Willow supple/stretchy, slimmer and more breathable than Strafe Scarlett Bib (more rugged). For those finding other shells bulky/stiff/sweaty.
- **Ortovox Ravine Free 3L Jacket & Pant** (overhauled) — $670 / $520; 593 g / 561 g (M); 3L OVX.Shield (recycled face). More distinctive freeride look, less logo-heavy, more relaxed fit; excellent protection in light fabric; soft thin wool backer; ample pockets, high-waist pant, velcro waist tabs; Ortovox runs small (size up if between) though less slim than before. Warmer/rugged → Ravine Free Plus.
- **Patagonia PowSlayer Jacket & Pant** — $799 / $649; 533 g / 511 g (M); 3L Gore-Tex Pro (80D recycled face). One of several brands using new PFAS-free Gore-Tex Pro this season — performs extremely similarly to last gen; among best for seriously wet weather; breathes better than standard Gore-Tex; light/packable yet resort-durable; somewhat stiff/crinkly. Softer → Rab Khroma Kinetic, Patagonia SnowDrifter; durability → Patagonia Untracked, Powder Town.
- **Rab Khroma Kinetic Jacket & Bib** — $400 / $380; 560 g / 579 g (size 10); 3L Proflex stretch (20D; claimed 20K). Optimizes breathability and ROM; feels like softshell — supple, stretchy; breathes better than most 3L shells — great for touring; OK for resort if not hard on gear; spring go-to; slim fit, fewer pockets.

**Male-reviewer tested**
- **Peak Performance Vertical Gore-Tex Pro Jacket & Bib** (new top-of-line) — $850 / $750; 686 g / 635 g (M); 3L Gore-Tex Pro (recycled nylon). PFAS-free Gore-Tex Pro; "freeride-inspired" long roomy yet sleek fit; many pockets, long underarm vents, excellent hood with tall standing collar; top-tier protection, breathes better than standard Gore-Tex; durable for daily resort; adequate for BC if prioritizing water resistance.
- **686 Smarty 3-in-1 Rodeo 3L Jacket** — $330; 719 g shell + 310 g vest (M); 3L infiniDRY (biobased face) + 200-g infiLOFT vest. Excellent value; burly fabric keeps wind/water out like pricier shells; warm light packable insulated vest; both parts work alone or together; large chest vents; breathes less than Vertical or air-permeable laminates; relaxed fit.
- **Flylow Quantum Pro Jacket & Baker Bib** — $440 / $440; 735 g (jacket L), 896 g (bib S); 3L Surface laminate (150D recycled). Mid Flylow lineup; class-leading durability over seasons; solid protection, decent breathability; roomy (esp. Baker Bib); tons of pockets/vents; not light/packable; survived hundreds of resort days in tight trees. New this season: Flylow Gore-Tex kits (Lab Gore-Tex Jacket, Baker Gore-Tex Bib) lighter with excellent protection. Bang-for-buck.
- **Trew PRIMO Cosmic Jacket & Eagle Pant** (women's: Stella Jacket & Olallie Pant) — $599 / $499; 906 g / 920 g (M); 3L PNW laminate (recycled face). Resort kit of choice for several reviewers; burly yet supple, not crinkly; 200+ days in Cosmic without a tear, still dry; Eagle Pant hasn't developed ski-brake cuff holes (reviewer normally destroys cuffs in 25-50 days); lots of pockets, loose relaxed fit; Eagle = lower-cut TREWth Bib PRIMO (bib coverage vs pant breathability).
- **Strafe Recon Softshell Pullover & Pant** — $269 / $269; 285 g / 373 g (M); Recon STRETCH softshell w/ brrr° tech. Only non-waterproof outerwear here — go-to touring kit for some; far more breathable; enough wind/light-snow protection; slim but not skimo-tight; minimalist useful features (interior skin drop-in pockets, dual electronics sleeves in pant hand pockets, real scuff guards); not for heavy precipitation. All-time favorite for mild BC days.
- **Patagonia Storm Shift Jacket & Pant** — $499 / $399; 802 g / 869 g (M); 2L Gore-Tex (recycled 150D face + fleece lining). Burly Gore-Tex with a bit of insulation (thin partial fleece like a low-profile R1 Air); warmth without being frigid-only (insulated version exists); more protective/abrasion-resistant than SnowDrifter, Stormstride, Upstride, PowSlayer; mid fit; rugged slightly warm resort shell.

### Helmets & Goggles (src ~14256-)
Section overview (Dylan Wood / Kristin Sinnott photos): Blister believes no one should ski/ride without a properly fitting helmet — speaking from experience: Jonathan Ellsworth fell while bootpacking on a summer tour, went over a cliff, tomahawked through boulders and broke his neck; wearing a helmet, he survived (he and witnesses believe he wouldn't have otherwise), had major surgery and skied six months later (Blister Podcast eps #47, #48). Not all helmets protect equally (GEAR:30 "Current State of Helmet Tech" series), but any is far better than none. Goggles: seeing well makes skiing safer and more fun; lens and lens-swap tech improving and trickling down to cheaper models.

**Helmets** (MSRP; measured weight)
- **Atomic Four Amid Pro** — $180; 487 g (M). Top-shelf features at mid price; "Amid" multiple dual-density pads deform to reduce multi-directional incl. rotational forces (Atomic claims up to 40% more protection than standards); magnetic FidLock buckle; soft antimicrobial liner; dial fit; few vents but good airflow moving; light — good 50/50 option. Ski mountaineering → Smith Summit, Salomon MTN Lab.
- **Giro Owen Spherical** — $240; 521 g (L). Flagship tech with clean minimal look; adaptable fit worked for very different heads; few vents but effective front/rear airflow; light; Mips Spherical via two-piece foam shell (quieter, comfier than thin plastic liner). More vents/less weight → Giro Grid; budget Mips → Giro Ledge.
- **Glade Boundary Mips** — $229; 421 g (M). Fully featured yet light; independently adjustable front/back vents; good for human-powered; dial works with mittens; magnetic Fidlock; Mips; flagship-level features at lower price. Fewer vents/cheaper → Glade Tenmile Mips.
- **POC Fornix Mips** — $230; 490 g (XL/XXL). Go-to for a reviewer: protection/weight/features/price; large adjustable vents, lift or BC; functional dial; traditional buckle; more padded interior recently. Similar category to Owen and Four Amid Pro. Rugged shell → POC Auric Cut.
- **Pret Vision X (women's) / Fury X (men's)** — $220; 473 g (M). Many features of flagship Cirque/Corona at lower price: ample venting, multi-shell w/ Mips, antimicrobial foam; Pret helmets (also tested Cirque X, Lyric X2, Cynic X2) pair with a broad range of goggles; Vision/Fury lighter/more ventilated than Lyric/Cynic (good values); all now with new "RCS360" single-dial fit system.
- **Salomon MTN Lab** — $290; 379 g (M). One of first BC-specific helmets, still a favorite; helped launch multi-norm category — ski, mountaineering, and bike certified; works with most goggles, looks normal; good for technical approaches with rock/ice fall; vents very well, thinner "summer" liner; lift-only users choose heavier. Alternatives: Smith Summit, Sweet Protection Ascender.
- **Smith Summit Mips** — $260; 404 g (M). Ski + mountaineering certified; BC features: headlamp routing, bungee for pack lashing, compact BOA, many vents; thin earpad-less knit liner — works with bare head, beanie, cap; low-profile coverage less ideal for resort (→ Smith Nexus, budget Method); doesn't pair well with some curved-top goggles; lighter, more breathable, better rockfall protection than most here in BC.
- **Sweet Protection Igniter 2Vi Mips** — $270; 721 g (M/L). Rugged; mid-lineup; "2Vi" structure: hard outer shell, shock-absorbing foam, 2-piece Mips liner integrated to cut weight and add comfort; dial fit, Fidlock buckle, multiple vents. Burlier → Trooper 2Vi; affordable/low-profile → Looper Mips.

**Goggles**
- **Anon M5 / M5.S** — $300 (2 Perceive lenses + MFI face mask). Anon helped pioneer magnetic lens swaps; M5 and smaller M5.S mid-size; "Flat Toric Lenses" (toric optics with sleeker cylindrical look); ridiculously easy, secure swaps; MFI face mask with magnetic nose piece. Cylindrical → new M6; same optics cheaper → Sync.
- **Dragon DX3 Plus OTG** — $160 (2 Lumalenses). Two high-end lenses affordably; traditional (less easy) swap vs RVX Mag; Lumalens contrast better than most cheap goggles; medium fit; "armored" venting resists clogging in storms; choose version with bright + low-light lens.
- **Glade Fathom+** — $199 (Reveal 4K photochromic + low-light lens). Versatility/value competitive with pricier goggles; quick magnetic swaps; new high-contrast, adaptable Reveal 4K photochromic; middle fit. Taller/longer face → Glade Adapt 2 ($169; no magnetic system but functional photochromic "set-and-forget").
- **Julbo Launcher Reactiv** — $290 (1 Reactiv lens). "SuperFlow Pro" frame lets lens pop out slightly for rapid venting; excellent Reactiv photochromic (tested "Reactiv 0-4 High Contrast") adapts to very wide light range incl. wet low-light storms; best with Reactiv (no easy swap system); larger frame but "medium" fit. Spherical → Julbo Lightyear.
- **Pit Viper Proform** — $170 (2 lenses). Affordable, eye-catching magnetic swap (yank and slap); Pit Viper's highest-end model; pricier goggles slightly better contrast (subtle); big tall frame, similar to Anon M4, fits M/L helmet wearers. Budget non-magnetic → Pit Viper French Fry ($70).
- **SHRED Gratify** — $220 (1 CBL 2.0 lens). Regular choice for several reviewers; proprietary "Contrast Boosting Lens 2.0" — single CBL 2.0 Ice lens gives excellent clarity/contrast sunny to graybird without swaps or photochromics; fairly large frame works for M helmets; no old-cylindrical distortion. GEAR:30 Ep. 272 with SHRED founder Ted Ligety (developed with MIT). Among best lenses for contrast/clarity/versatility.
- **Smith Squad XL Imprint 3D** — $250 (2 ChromaPop + custom 3D-printed frame). Most innovative: custom frame 3D-printed from at-home phone face scan — reviewer's best-fitting goggle ever; no quick swap; broad coverage for medium-large faces. Standard Squad XL ($163) top value pick; I/O Mag Imprint 3D for easy swapping.
- **SPY Marauder Elite Happy Boost** — $340 (2 Happy Boost lenses). Happy Boost among best optics tested — color contrast/saturation better than most high-end goggles; toric lens; six-magnet quick swap; medium-large frame, thicker frame/foam may protrude from thin-brim helmets. Cheaper SPY models now also offer Happy Boost.

## 3. Non-product articles
(Editorial framing, How-to-use, Spec interpretation and Letter from the Editor are summarized in section 1; Ski-Quiver Selections article is summarized in place before Ski Boots; section overviews for boots/bindings/apparel/helmets — incl. boot-fitting advice and Ellsworth's helmet story — are under each category.)

### Blister 'Best Of' Awards (src ~14554-14936)
Overview (Luke Koppa / Paul Forward photos): singles out exceptional products — personal favorites, though other gear might fit you better. Rules: (1) between similar performers, pick the one with most time logged — not about new, about good; (2) only gear Blister can vouch for (criticizes publications that sell awards or award barely-tested gear); (3) no company paid for inclusion or awards.

- **All-Mountain Skis**: Folsom Spar 88 (favorite sub-90 mm true all-mountain); K2 Mindbender 89Ti (exciting carver with off-piste capability); Salomon QST 94 & 100 (forgiving, playful, yet hard-skiable); Line Blade (odd, oddly fun, adaptable); Line Optic 88, 96, 104 & 114 (suspension + freestyle playfulness); K2 Mindbender 99Ti (well-rounded, not boring); J Skis Masterblaster (damp, smooth, not demanding); Völkl Mantra 102 (upper echelon suspension/composure yet accessible); Icelantic Nomad 100, 106 & 112 (easygoing, playful, composed at speed); RMU Apostle 106 & 114 (most playful/maneuverable, not niche); Moment Countach 110 (nearly all reviewers enjoy it).
- **Freestyle Skis**: Black Crows Mirus Cor (makes no sense on paper; all-time favorite for low-angle groomers/features); Season Kin (tight-turning, soft, playful); Faction Studio 2 (stout, balanced, lively); K2 Reckoner 102 & Reckoner KF (extremely playful yet predictable); Rossignol Sender Free 110 (freestyle shape with damp stable charger guts, more precision than 118); Dynastar M-Free 112 (more forgiving/playful, still damp; 190 cm a new favorite "playful charger").
- **Powder Skis**: Prior CBC (steep pillow lines); Rossignol Sender Free 118 (managing editor's all-time favorite); Folsom Rapture (Paul Forward's AK guiding pow ski).
- **Women-Specific Skis**: Elan Ripstick 94 W; Blizzard Black Pearl 94; Völkl Mantra 84 W, M7 Mantra W & Mantra 102 W (formerly Secret 84/96/102); K2 Mindbender 99Ti W ("this ski just works"); Icelantic Maiden 102 & 108; Wagner Summit 106 (all reviewers, female and male, got along immediately; excellent 50/50); Coalition Snow Rafiki (all-time favorite resort pow ski).
- **50/50 Skis**: Atomic Bent 110 & Bent Chetler 120 (lightweight skis minimizing downsides of low mass); 4FRNT Hoji (adaptable).
- **Touring Skis**: Majesty Superwolf (spring couloirs, crust, hot pow); 4FRNT Raven (predictability in unpredictable BC snow); Heritage Lab BC120 (rare excellent ~120 mm touring ski).
- **Touring Bindings**: ATK Raider 12 (benchmark of 300-400 g class).
- **Ski Boots**: Tecnica Zero G Tour Pro & Tour Pro W; Atomic Hawx Ultra XTD 130 & 115 W; Tecnica Cochise 130 & 115 W; Scarpa F1 LT; Tecnica Zero G Peak Carbon; Salomon S/Lab MTN Summit; Atomic Hawx Ultra 130; Lange Shadow 130 LV & Shadow 115 LV W; ZipFit Liners (re-customizable cork; nearly indestructible; some reviewer pairs past 500 days, outlasting boot shells).
- **Snowboarding**: Weston Japow snowboard; Voile Skyline splitboard; Venture Paragon splitboard; Cardiff Powgoda Pro Carbon splitboard; Jones Ultralight Hovercraft 2.0 splitboard; Spark Surge ST Pro & Arc ST Pro split bindings; Jones Mercury FASE snowboard binding.
- **Apparel — Outerwear**: Trew PRIMO Cosmic & Stella Jackets & Eagle Pants; Flylow Baker Bib (probably most durable ski outerwear tested; now also Gore-Tex version); Ortovox Ravine Free kit (great 50/50); Strafe Recon Pullover & Pant.
- **Apparel — Midlayers** (not reviewed elsewhere in guide): Patagonia Micro Puff & DAS Light Hoodies (PlumaFill warmth-to-weight, water resistant "just in case" insulators); The North Face Summit Series Pumori Down Parka (extremely warm, oversized, packable, giant hood); Patagonia Nano-Air series (active insulation pioneer; Nano-Air Jacket for resort; new Nano-Air Ultralight Freeride Hoody for touring).
- **Apparel — Baselayers**: CW-X Stabilyx Joint Support ¾ Compression Tight (knee support; worn nearly daily by a reviewer); Le Bent Heavyweight Range Crew & Bottom (merino/bamboo-rayon, grid knit; good for wool-sensitive); Nuyarn Wool baselayers (merino benefits with much better durability).
- **Goggles**: Anon WM1, M2, M3, M4, M5 & Sync; Glade Adapt 2; Julbo Launcher REACTIV; SPY Marauder Elite x Happy Boost.
- **Accessories**: Scott Team Issue Pole (still unbroken after 10+ years and multiple pairs); GoggleSocs; DB Ski Bag (favorite 10+ years; light, rolls up, shoulder strap for hands-free wheeling); Skida Brim & Running Hats; Wells Lamont HydraHyde Mittens (all-leather, cheap, durable, wrist leash — maybe best value in snowsports); Hestra Leather Fall Line Gloves (hundreds of days; for drier locales); BCA Float E2-35 (best all-round avalanche airbag pack tested; supercapacitor Alpride E2; "Side Stash" pockets, radio spot); Patagonia PowSlayer Pack (sleek, functional ski/helmet carry); Mammut Barryvox S2 beacon (Paul Forward's all-time favorite; current version even better).

### Index of Skis & Credits (src ~14938-15127)
- Index of skis by brand → model → page (pp. 287-289), covering every ski above (many appear in multiple sections; Best Of page references 280-282). No new content.
- Thank-you page: to BLISTER+ members (support makes project possible; commitment to truth and rejecting revenue that undermines honesty); reviewers (Kristin Sinnott, Dylan Wood, Paul Forward, Kara Williard, Andrew Forward, Jed Doane, Justin Bobb, Mark Danielson, Noah Eckhouse, Sascha Anastas, Luke Koppa, Jonathan Ellsworth); photographers (Taylor Ahearn, Charlie Renfro, Mary Albertolle, Riley Ebel, Jeff Cricco, Katie Botwin, Zach Husted, and staff); staff: Luke Koppa (Managing Editor & Graphic Designer), Dylan Wood (Senior Editor), Mark Danielson (Copy Editor), Kristin Sinnott (Senior Editor), Jonathan Ellsworth (Editor-in-Chief). Closing photo pages.

## 4. Notable themes / what's new this year
- **Brand overhauls and renames**: Völkl renamed all metal-laminate all-mountain skis "Mantra" (Kanjo 84 → Mantra 84; Kendo 88 → Mantra 88; Secret 84/96/102 → Mantra 84 W / M7 Mantra W / Mantra 102 W; Yumi 84 → Mantra 84 W; Katana 108 → Mantra 108); Head's entire Kore line rebuilt as "Kore Ti" (heavier, softer, more rockered, two titanal layers — much better suspension; "biggest makeover"); Salomon overhauled QST (QST 94 & 100 new, unisex labeling; QST 106 and QST Blank revised — lighter/looser); Dynastar M-Free 100 replaces M-Free 99 (Hybrid Core 2.0); Armada ARW 106 → unisex ARV 106; K2 Omen Team replaces Poacher.
- **New skis of note**: Völkl Revolt 101 (directional, category-defying), Peregrine 72, Atomic Remedy 130 boot, Faction Dancer 79, Rossignol Sender Free 100 and Arcade 94, Majesty HNX Ti and Vanguard 108 Ti, Moment Siren 104 (women's Countach), Coalition Lil' Nasty, Liberty Radian 106, RMU Professor 101, Blizzard Zero G 96 (redesign), Fischer RC4 Noize, La Sportiva Tempo (lightened), Weston Dream Machine & Gnarnia boards, K2 Sky Pilot, Never Summer Proto Type 3.
- **Weight-vs-stability debate**: Blister repeatedly stresses mass correlates with stability; brands are rediscovering weight's value (Chargers section; Head Kore Ti heavier); yet light builds (Renoun VibeStop, Katana V-Werks, Kaizen, Superwolf) praised for punching above weight. Lighter skis no longer automatically in "More Forgiving" sections.
- **Category blurring**: directional vs playful lines blur (Revolt 101, Countach 104/110, Declivity X, M-Free 112 in multiple sections); many skis appear in 2-3 sections; "playful charger" concept (Wildcat 118, Sender Free 118, M-Free 112).
- **Fun carvers**: unconventional tight-radius all-mountain carvers (Line Blade, Black Crows Mirus Cor/Octo, Folsom Spar Turbo, ZAG Mata Ti, Line Sakana, Season Kin) prominent and award-winning.
- **Boots**: wave of dual-BOA and buckle-less boots (Head Kaliber, Salomon S/Pro Supra Dual BOA, K2 Cortex 140 dual-BOA); hybrid shells (Armada AR ONE, Phaenom FS 01); fixed-cuff boots with tech toes for hybrid bindings (K2 Recon 140, Fischer RC4 Free Pro); Rossignol Vizion step-in/out entry; ZipFit liners lauded; Blister says BOA shouldn't decide purchases but lower BOA generally preferred.
- **Hybrid touring bindings mature**: Tyrolia enters with Attack Hybrid 14; Shift2 fixes Shift's issues; CAST Freetour 2.0 refined; Tyrolia Almonte debut in light tech class; ATK Raider remains benchmark.
- **Women's gear**: big women's sections with 3 subclasses; emphasis on women's skis that don't compromise high-speed stability (Santa Ana, Black Pearl 94, Mantra W); women's touring boots (Zero G Tour Pro W stiffest).
- **Snowboards**: volume-shifted/short-wide and swallowtail surf shapes dominate (Excavator, Special Effects, Storm Chaser, Warpig/Superpig), plus quick-entry bindings (Jones FASE, Nidecker Supermatic).
- **Sustainability/materials**: PFAS-free Gore-Tex Pro (Patagonia, Peak Performance), Fischer Curv GT 85 Redefine (−36% CO2e), Jones Re-Up recycled-board stringers, Ferreol flax instead of fiberglass, recycled PET stringers in Völkl Blaze; editor urges not buying gear you don't need.
- **Blister business framing**: heavy promotion of BLISTER+ (insurance testimonials, personalized recs), Blister Summit 2026 (April 13-17), GEAR:30 podcast cross-references; strong editorial independence messaging (no manufacturer ad money, no paid awards).
- **Editor's letter**: theme of beginner's mind — Ellsworth taking up snowboarding, flat pedals, gravel racing.
